0.0mu$\crcr\kern-1.29167pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle C^{\prime}D^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}>\pi, so we need to exclude this situation to establish uniqueness.
This is another reason to require the convex face condition, which ensures that all edge lengths are less than  \pi π\pi.
In fact, we establish that if the interior angles of a spherical quadrilateral lie within the range  (0,\pi)\cup(\pi,2\pi) (0,π)∪(π,2​π)(0,\pi)\cup(\pi,2\pi), and the edge lengths lie within  (0,\pi) (0,π)(0,\pi), then the quadrilateral is uniquely determined by all its interior angles and one edge length. These conditions are guaranteed by the requirements of strictly-convex faces and non-flat edges.





5.4 Rigidity for spherical quadrilaterals




Finally, we prove a rigidity theorem for spherical quadrilaterals, which is a generalization of Theorem 2.10. The idea of the proof is quite straightforward: we examine all possible constructions under the given constraints, and then confirm that no simultaneous realizations can occur among them.



Theorem 5.32.



Let  (v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) (v1​v2​v3​v4)(v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) be a spherical quadrilateral, possibly nonconvex and self-intersecting, that satisfies the following conditions: (i) all edge lengths are less than  \pi π\pi, and (ii) all interior angles are not singular.
Under these conditions, the spherical quadrilateral is uniquely determined by one edge length and all interior angles.




Proof.



Let  \theta_{i} θi\theta_{i} be the interior angle at  v_{i} viv_{i} for  i=1,\dots,4 i=1,…,4i=1,\dots,4.
Without loss of generality, we assume that the length of the edge 


 \mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu # #\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu

 \hfil\textstyle v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\hfil v1​v2\hfil\textstyle v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\hfil
 is given and there remains essentially three cases as follows,

1. 



 \theta_{1}<\pi θ1<π\theta_{1}<\pi and  \theta_{2}<\pi θ2<π\theta_{2}<\pi ,

2. 



 \theta_{1}<\pi θ1<π\theta_{1}<\pi and  \theta_{2}>\pi θ2>π\theta_{2}>\pi without self-intersection,

3. 



 \theta_{1}<\pi θ1<π\theta_{1}<\pi and  \theta_{2}>\pi θ2>π\theta_{2}>\pi with self-intersection.






In case (i), we consider drawing a geodesic with interior angle  \theta_{i} θi\theta_{i} to the edge 


 \mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu # #\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu

 \hfil\textstyle v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\hfil v1​v2\hfil\textstyle v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\hfil
 at each  v_{i} viv_{i} for  i=1,2 i=1,2i=1,2. Each geodesic forms a great circle on  \mathbb{S}^{2} 𝕊2\mathbb{S}^{2} and intersects each other twice. We denote the first intersecting point as  v_{5} v5v_{5}. Each great circle is divided into two geodesic segments by  v_{1} v1v_{1} (or  v_{2} v2v_{2}) and  v_{5} v5v_{5}.
The vertex  v_{3} v3v_{3} (or  v_{4} v4v_{4}) can be placed on one of the two geodesic segments. We denote the position of  v_{3} v3v_{3} (or  v_{4} v4v_{4}) as  y yy and  y^{\prime} y′y^{\prime} (or  x xx and  x^{\prime} x′x^{\prime}) as shown in Figure 18. . For example, if we choose  y^{\prime} y′y^{\prime} and  x xx for  v_{3} v3v_{3} and  v_{4} v4v_{4}, respectively, then  (v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4})=(v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{5})\cup(v_{5}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) (v1​v2​v3​v4)=(v1​v2​v5)∪(v5​v3​v4)(v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4})=(v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{5})\cup(v_{5}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}), as illustrated in Figure 18.
The triangle  (v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}) (v1​v2​v5)(v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}) is uniquely determined by  ASA A​S​AASA condition of Corollary 5.27 because all edge lengths are less than  \pi π\pi. Similarly, the triangle  (v_{5}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) (v5​v3​v4)(v_{5}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) is also uniquely determined by  AAA A​A​AAAA condition because all interior angles are less than  \pi π\pi.




Let us consider case (ii) where  \theta_{1}<\pi θ1<π\theta_{1}<\pi and  \theta_{2}>\pi θ2>π\theta_{2}>\pi, without self-intersection. Now, let us consider case (d) as the only possible configuration for case (iii).
We extend the two edges

 \hfil\textstyle v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}\hfil v1​v4\hfil\textstyle v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}\hfil
 and 


 \mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu # #\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu

 \hfil\textstyle v_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\hfil v3​v2\hfil\textstyle v_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\hfil
 in the right directions as illustrated in Figure 20 (d). The two geodesics intersect twice, and we choose the first intersection point as the new vertex  v_{5} v5v_{5}.
The triangle  (v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}) (v1​v2​v5)(v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}) is uniquely determined by  ASA A​S​AASA condition of Proposition 5.27 since the length of 


 \mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu # #\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu

 \hfil\textstyle v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}\hfil v1​v5\hfil\textstyle v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}\hfil
 is less than  \pi π\pi. The triangle  (v_{4}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}) (v4​v3​v5)(v_{4}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}) is also uniquely determined by  AAA A​A​AAAA condition since the length of 


 \mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu # #\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu

 \hfil\textstyle v_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}\hfil v3​v4\hfil\textstyle v_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}\hfil
 is less than  \pi π\pi.
By the uniqueness of the two triangles  (v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}) (v1​v2​v5)(v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}) and  (v_{4}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}) (v4​v3​v5)(v_{4}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{5}), we obtain the uniqueness of  (v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) (v1​v2​v3​v4)(v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) for case (iii). Note that we deduce that  \theta_{4}<\pi θ4<π\theta_{4}<\pi and  \theta_{3}>\pi θ3>π\theta_{3}>\pi for case (iii).




To complete the proof, we have to check possibility of simultaneous realizations of case (ii) and case (iii).
We will demonstrate that  \theta_{3} θ3\theta_{3} or  \theta_{4} θ4\theta_{4} must differ, thus preventing simultaneous realization of cases (ii) and (iii).
For case (ii)-(b), we remove the hemisphere and reconsider the resulting quadrilateral  (v_{1}\mskip 1.0mu\widehat{v_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) (v1​v2​v3^​v4)(v_{1}\mskip 1.0mu\widehat{v_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}). The three interior angles of  (v_{1}\mskip 1.0mu\widehat{v_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) (v1​v2​v3^​v4)(v_{1}\mskip 1.0mu\widehat{v_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) at  v_{1} v1v_{1},  v_{2} v2v_{2}, and  v_{3} v3v_{3} all are less than  \pi π\pi. If  \theta_{4}<\pi θ4<π\theta_{4}<\pi then all edge lengths of the quadrilateral are less than  \pi π\pi according to Corollary 2.9.
Then, the original edge length 


 \mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu # #\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-7.0mu}\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-1.0mu}$}}{\hfill}\mathrel{\mkern 0.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu

 \hfil\textstyle v_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\hfil v2​v3\hfil\textstyle v_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\hfil
 becomes greater than  \pi π\pi and it contradicts the assumption that all edge lengths are less than  \pi π\pi.
Therefore, it must be  \theta_{4}>\pi θ4>π\theta_{4}>\pi: simultaneous realization of (ii)-(a) and (iii) is impossible.
In case of (ii)-(c), we have already shown that  \theta_{3}<\pi θ3<π\theta_{3}<\pi, making simultaneous realization with (iii) impossible.
As a result, we conclude that simultaneous realization of case (ii) and case (iii) is completely impossible.




Considering all the discussed points, we have ruled out all possibilities for non-unique realizations. This ultimately proves the uniqueness of the quadrilateral  (v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}) (v1​v2​v3​v4)(v_{1}\mskip 1.0muv_{2}\mskip 1.0muv_{3}\mskip 1.0muv_{4}).
∎





We may extend the same theorem to a general  n nn-gon with  n>4 n>4n>4. Providing a rigorous proof is, however, anticipated to be quite challenging.
Since it’s not critical to our main result of establishing the rigidity of 3-dimensional polyhedra, we have not addressed this issue in the present paper. However, we recognize this question as significant in its own right and leave it as an open problem, as stated in Conjecture 2.
